worrying once it starts, even when I tell myself I’ve made the list, checked the dates, and done all I reasonably can.\n- key: S058\n  name: Restlessness_Tension\n  description: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.\n  manifestation: I often feel wound up in my own living room, tapping my fingers during phone calls or getting up to straighten something just because sitting quietly it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is that I can’t seem to turn off the worrying once it gets going. I’ll tell myself, “All right, I’ve made the list, I checked the dates, I did what I could,” and then somehow my mind just keeps circling back anyway.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to be very organized—color-coded calendars, little notes on the fridge, all that—and I still do those things, but lately it doesn’t seem to settle me the way it used to. I know I’ve done what’s reasonable, but I can’t quite make myself stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts keep coming back, do they feel unwanted and intrusive, like they pop into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so—well, maybe “intrusive” is the right word, though it’s not like a flash of something bizarre. It’s more that once the worry starts, I can’t seem to shut it off. I’ll say to myself, I made the list, I checked the dates, I’ve done everything reasonable… and then there it is again, circling back.\n\nI used to keep everything very tidy that way—my husband used to tease me about my little notes—but now the notes don’t really quiet it down. I’ll say to myself, I made the list, I checked the dates, I’ve done everything reasonable… and then there it is again, circling back.\n\nI used to keep everything very tidy that way—my husband used to tease me about my little notes—but now the notes don’t really quiet it down. It’s unwanted, yes. I don’t feel like I’m choosing to keep thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: When the worry comes back, do you do something over and over to try to feel better, like checking, repeating, or counting?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I do counting or repeating exactly. I mean, I do look back at my calendar sometimes, or I’ll glance at a note again, but that’s sort of what I’ve always done to keep myself organized.\n\nMy daughter bought me this very pretty planner last Christmas—one of those with stickers in the back, which seems a little silly at my age, but I actually liked it—and I’ll check it, yes, but I’m not sure it’s “over and over” in the way you mean. It’s more like I’m trying to reassure myself I haven’t missed something.\n\nDoctor: Are you worrying about lots of different parts of your life, or mainly about one particular problem?\n\nPatient: It’s not just one thing, no. It sort of wanders. I’ll start thinking about the exhibition schedule—there’s a little community art thing I’ve been helping keep track of—and then somehow I’m worrying about my daughter’s travel plans, whether she’ll get stuck somewhere, and then the gutters on the house because I noticed leaves up there the other day.\n\nIt’s like each thing by itself is small, but in my head it starts to feel as if it could turn into a much bigger mess if I don’t stay on top of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I think it started around her death, yes, but now it doesn’t always feel tied to that directly. It’s more like my mind just keeps roaming—from the exhibition schedule to my daughter’s travel plans to whether the gutters need fixing.

I’ll start thinking about the exhibition schedule—there’s a little community art thing I’ve been helping keep track of—and then somehow I’m worrying about my daughter’s travel plans, whether she’ll get stuck somewhere, and then the gutters on the house because I noticed leaves up there the other day.\n\nIt’s like each thing by itself is small, but in my head it starts to feel as if it could turn into a much bigger mess if I don’t stay on top of it.\n\nDoctor: Did this worrying start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it did. She was always the one who remembered who needed to be called, which folder had what in it—funny the things you miss. But after that, the worrying just seemed to settle in and not really let up.\n\nDoctor: When the worry hits, do you feel driven to check your planner or notes again even when you know you’ve already checked enough?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “driven,” exactly. Sometimes I do look again, but it’s hard to tell where being careful ends and… well, where it becomes too much.\n\nI’ve always been a notes person. Even in college I had those color-coded tabs—though now I can never find the color I want, which is ridiculous because I bought a whole pack of them. But yes, I’ll sometimes look back at the planner after I’ve already looked. I don’t always feel like I know whether that’s because I need to, or because I just don’t trust my memory in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel mostly connected to your friend’s death, or do they now feel separate from it?\n\nPatient: I think it started around her death, yes, but now it doesn’t always feel tied to that directly. It’s more like my mind just keeps roaming—from the exhibition schedule to my daughter’s travel plans to whether the gutters need fixing.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, well, if I don’t keep an eye on this one little thing, maybe it turns into a much bigger mess. Which sounds silly when I say it, because gutters are gutters, not… I don’t know, a catastrophe. But in my head they all start to feel connected somehow, like things could unravel if I’m not careful.\n\nDoctor: For the past six months or more, have you been worrying on most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I’d say. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.2, F42.9, F41.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is that I can’t seem to turn off the worrying once it gets going. I’ll tell myself, “All right, I’ve made the list, I checked the dates, I did what I could,” and then somehow my mind just keeps circling back anyway.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to be very organized—color-coded calendars, little notes on the fridge, all that—and I still do those things, but lately it doesn’t seem to settle me the way it used to. I know I’ve done what’s reasonable, but I can’t quite make myself stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts keep coming back, do they feel unwanted and intrusive, like they pop into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so—well, maybe “intrusive” is the right word, though it’s not like a flash of something bizarre. It’s more that once the worry starts, I can’t seem to shut it off. I’ll say to myself, I made the list, I checked the dates, I’ve done everything reasonable… and then there it is again, circling back.\n\nI used to keep everything very tidy that way—my husband used to tease me about my little notes—but now the notes don’t really quiet it down. It’s unwanted, yes. I don’t feel like I’m choosing to keep thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: When the worry comes back, do you do something over and over to try to feel better, like checking, repeating, or counting?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I do counting or repeating exactly. I mean, I do look back at my calendar sometimes, or I’ll glance at a note again, but that’s sort of what I’ve always done to keep myself organized.\n\nMy daughter bought me this very pretty planner last Christmas—one of those with stickers in the back, which seems a little silly at my age, but I actually liked it—and I’ll check it, yes, but I’m not sure it’s “over and over” in the way you mean. It’s more like I’m trying to reassure myself I haven’t missed something.\n\nDoctor: Are you worrying about lots of different parts of your life, or mainly about one particular problem?\n\nPatient: It’s not just one thing, no. It sort of wanders. I’ll start thinking about the exhibition schedule—there’s a little community art thing I’ve been helping keep track of—and then somehow I’m worrying about my daughter’s travel plans, whether she’ll get stuck somewhere, and then the gutters on the house because I noticed leaves up there the other day.\n\nIt’s like each thing by itself is small, but in my head it starts to feel as if it could turn into a much bigger mess if I don’t stay on top of it.\n\nDoctor: Did this worrying start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it did. It’s been about… seven months now, give or take. It started not long after my friend died. Even in college I had those color-coded tabs—though now I can never find the color I want, which is ridiculous because I bought a whole pack of them. But yes, I’ll sometimes look back at the planner after I’ve already looked. I don’t always feel like I know whether that’s because I need to, or because I just don’t trust my memory in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel mostly connected to your friend’s death, or do they now feel separate from it?\n\nPatient: I think it started around her death, yes, but now it doesn’t always feel tied to that directly. It’s more like my mind just keeps roaming—from the exhibition schedule to my daughter’s travel plans to whether the gutters need fixing.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, well, if I don’t keep an eye on this one little thing, maybe it turns into a much bigger mess. Which sounds silly when I say it, because gutters are gutters, not… I don’t know, a catastrophe. But in my head they all start to feel connected somehow, like things could unravel if I’m not careful.\n\nDoctor: For the past six months or more, have you been worrying on most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I’d say. It’s not always the same worry, which makes it hard to pin down. My mind just sort of roams—one minute it’s the exhibition schedule, then it’s my daughter’s travel plans, then I’m thinking about whether the house gutters need fixing.\n\nAnd it starts small, but then I get this feeling that if I don’t stay on top of it, it could turn into something much bigger. Like things could unravel. I know that sounds a bit dramatic when I hear myself say it.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to control or stop it?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s really the thing that made me come in. Once it starts, I have a very hard time shutting it off.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “You’ve made the list, you checked the dates, you’ve done what you can,” and for a little while that sounds reasonable. But then my mind sort of circles back anyway. It’s like when I used to pack for trips and I’d check the stove twice—although now I’m not even traveling much, so that’s not quite the same—but it’s that same feeling of not being able to leave it alone. Even when I know, logically, there isn’t anything more to do.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. She was always the one who remembered who needed to be called, which folder had what in it—funny the things you miss. But after that, the worrying just seemed to settle in and not really let up.\n\nDoctor: When the worry hits, do you feel driven to check your planner or notes again even when you know you’ve already checked enough?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “driven,” exactly. Sometimes I do look again, but it’s hard to tell where being careful ends and… well, where it becomes too much.\n\nI’ve always been a notes person. Even in college I had those color-coded tabs—though now I can never find the color I want, which is ridiculous because I bought a whole pack of them. But yes, I’ll sometimes look back at the planner after I’ve already looked. I don’t always feel like I know whether that’s because I need to, or because I just don’t trust my memory in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel mostly connected to your friend’s death, or do they now feel separate from it?\n\nPatient: I think it started around her death, yes, but now it doesn’t always feel tied to that directly. It’s more like my mind just keeps roaming—from the exhibition schedule to my daughter’s travel plans to whether the gutters need fixing.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, well, if I don’t keep an eye on this one little thing, maybe it turns into a much bigger mess. Which sounds silly when I say it, because gutters are gutters, not… I don’t know, a catastrophe. But in my head they all start to feel connected somehow, like things could unravel if I’m not careful.\n\nDoctor: For the past six months or more, have you been worrying on most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I’d say. It’s not always the same worry, which makes it hard to pin down. My mind just sort of roams—one minute it’s the exhibition schedule, then it’s my daughter’s travel plans, then I’m thinking about whether the house gutters need fixing.\n\nAnd it starts small, but then I get this feeling that if I don’t stay on top of it, it could turn into something much bigger. Like things could unravel. I know that sounds a bit dramatic when I hear myself say it.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to control or stop it?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s really the thing that made me come in. Once it starts, I have a very hard time shutting it off.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “You’ve made the list, you checked the dates, you’ve done what you can,” and for a little while that sounds reasonable. But then my mind sort of circles back anyway. It’s like when I used to pack for trips and I’d check the stove twice—although now I’m not even traveling much, so that’s not quite the same—but it’s that same feeling of not being able to leave it alone. Even when I know, logically, there isn’t anything more to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Not pacing the halls exactly, but I do feel… wound up, even just sitting in my own living room.\n\nI’ll be on the phone and notice I’m tapping my fingers, or I’ll get up to straighten a stack of papers or move a cushion, not because it really needs doing. It’s more that sitting quietly makes me feel jumpy. Funny, I used to enjoy just sitting with a cup of tea and looking out at the garden, and now I’ll suddenly think the books on the side table look crooked. But yes—on edge is probably the right word.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I know I’ve done what’s reasonable, but I can’t quite make myself stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts keep coming back, do they feel unwanted and intrusive, like they pop into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so—well, maybe “intrusive” is the right word, though it’s not like a flash of something bizarre. I know that sounds a bit dramatic when I hear myself say it.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to control or stop it?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s really the thing that made me come in. Once it starts, I have a very hard time shutting it off.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “You’ve made the list, you checked the dates, you’ve done what you can,” and for a little while that sounds reasonable. But then my mind sort of circles back anyway. It’s like when I used to pack for trips and I’d check the stove twice—although now I’m not even traveling much, so that’s not quite the same—but it’s that same feeling of not being able to leave it alone. Even when I know, logically, there isn’t anything more to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Not pacing the halls exactly, but I do feel… wound up, even just sitting in my own living room.\n\nI’ll be on the phone and notice I’m tapping my fingers, or I’ll get up to straighten a stack of papers or move a cushion, not because it really needs doing. It’s more that sitting quietly makes me feel jumpy. Funny, I used to enjoy just sitting with a cup of tea and looking out at the garden, and now I’ll suddenly think the books on the side table look crooked. But yes—on edge is probably the right word.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get tired easily?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even if I’ve slept right through the night, I’ll wake up feeling like I’ve already done half a day’s work, which is ridiculous because I’ve only made tea and fed myself breakfast.\n\nAnd then little things tire me out more than they used to. I’ll water the garden beds—nothing strenuous, just moving the hose about—or I’ll sort some papers for book club, and afterward I need to sit down and sort of… gather myself. I used to be able to do those things without even thinking about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I read the newspaper most mornings, and sometimes I realize I’ve read the same paragraph twice, but then again the paper is so full of the same unpleasant stories lately that maybe I’m just avoiding it.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is that I can’t seem to turn off the worrying once it gets going. I’ll tell myself, “All right, I’ve made the list, I checked the dates, I did what I could,” and then somehow my mind just keeps circling back anyway.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to be very organized—color-coded calendars, little notes on the fridge, all that—and I still do those things, but lately it doesn’t seem to settle me the way it used to. I know I’ve done what’s reasonable, but I can’t quite make myself stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts keep coming back, do they feel unwanted and intrusive, like they pop into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so—well, maybe “intrusive” is the right word, though it’s not like a flash of something bizarre. It’s more that once the worry starts, I can’t seem to shut it off. I’ll say to myself, I made the list, I checked the dates, I’ve done everything reasonable… and then there it is again, circling back.\n\nI used to keep everything very tidy that way—my husband used to tease me about my little notes—but now the notes don’t really quiet it down. It’s unwanted, yes. I don’t feel like I’m choosing to keep thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: When the worry comes back, do you do something over and over to try to feel better, like checking, repeating, or counting?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I do counting or repeating exactly. I mean, I do look back at my calendar sometimes, or I’ll glance at a note again, but that’s sort of what I’ve always done to keep myself organized.\n\nMy daughter bought me this very pretty planner last Christmas—one of those with stickers in the back, which seems a little silly at my age, but I actually liked it—and I’ll check it, yes, but I’m not sure it’s “over and over” in the way you mean. It’s more like I’m trying to reassure myself I haven’t missed something.\n\nDoctor: Are you worrying about lots of different parts of your life, or mainly about one particular problem?\n\nPatient: It’s not just one thing, no. It sort of wanders. I’ll start thinking about the exhibition schedule—there’s a little community art thing I’ve been helping keep track of—and then somehow I’m worrying about my daughter’s travel plans, whether she’ll get stuck somewhere, and then the gutters on the house because I noticed leaves up there the other day.\n\nIt’s like each thing by itself is small, but in my head it starts to feel as if it could turn into a much bigger mess if I don’t stay on top of it.\n\nDoctor: Did this worrying start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it did. It’s been about… seven months now, give or take. It started not long after my friend died. We had been working on a few things together—little projects, nothing grand, but they mattered to us—and I sort of tried to keep them moving after she was gone.\n\nAt first I thought I was just being responsible, you know, keeping lists and not letting things drop. She was always the one who remembered who needed to be called, which folder had what in it—funny the things you miss. But after that, the worrying just seemed to settle in and not really let up.\n\nDoctor: When the worry hits, do you feel driven to check your planner or notes again even when you know you’ve already checked enough?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “driven,” exactly. Sometimes I do look again, but it’s hard to tell where being careful ends and… well, where it becomes too much.\n\nI’ve always been a notes person. Even in college I had those color-coded tabs—though now I can never find the color I want, which is ridiculous because I bought a whole pack of them. But yes, I’ll sometimes look back at the planner after I’ve already looked. I don’t always feel like I know whether that’s because I need to, or because I just don’t trust my memory in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel mostly connected to your friend’s death, or do they now feel separate from it?\n\nPatient: I think it started around her death, yes, but now it doesn’t always feel tied to that directly. It’s more like my mind just keeps roaming—from the exhibition schedule to my daughter’s travel plans to whether the gutters need fixing.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, well, if I don’t keep an eye on this one little thing, maybe it turns into a much bigger mess. Which sounds silly when I say it, because gutters are gutters, not… I don’t know, a catastrophe. But in my head they all start to feel connected somehow, like things could unravel if I’m not careful.\n\nDoctor: For the past six months or more, have you been worrying on most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I’d say. It’s not always the same worry, which makes it hard to pin down. Like things could unravel. But then my mind sort of circles back anyway. Funny, I used to enjoy just sitting with a cup of tea and looking out at the garden, and now I’ll suddenly think the books on the side table look crooked. But yes—on edge is probably the right word.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get tired easily?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even if I’ve slept right through the night, I’ll wake up feeling like I’ve already done half a day’s work, which is ridiculous because I’ve only made tea and fed myself breakfast.\n\nAnd then little things tire me out more than they used to. I’ll water the garden beds—nothing strenuous, just moving the hose about—or I’ll sort some papers for book club, and afterward I need to sit down and sort of… gather myself. I used to be able to do those things without even thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I read the newspaper most mornings, and sometimes I realize I’ve read the same paragraph twice, but then again the paper is so full of the same unpleasant stories lately that maybe I’m just avoiding it.\n\nI haven’t noticed it as a major thing, I don’t think. If I’m doing something straightforward, like making a grocery list or checking the mail, I manage. Though I did put the stamps in the wrong drawer last week—but that’s hardly new for me. My sister used to tease me about that years ago. So… I suppose I’d say not especially, unless you mean something more specific.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Yes, especially by the end of the day. My shoulders and neck get terribly tight, like I’ve been holding myself braced without realizing it.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is that I can’t seem to turn off the worrying once it gets going. I’ll tell myself, “All right, I’ve made the list, I checked the dates, I did what I could,” and then somehow my mind just keeps circling back anyway.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to be very organized—color-coded calendars, little notes on the fridge, all that—and I still do those things, but lately it doesn’t seem to settle me the way it used to. I know I’ve done what’s reasonable, but I can’t quite make myself stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts keep coming back, do they feel unwanted and intrusive, like they pop into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so—well, maybe “intrusive” is the right word, though it’s not like a flash of something bizarre. It’s more that once the worry starts, I can’t seem to shut it off. I’ll say to myself, I made the list, I checked the dates, I’ve done everything reasonable… and then there it is again, circling back.\n\nI used to keep everything very tidy that way—my husband used to tease me about my little notes—but now the notes don’t really quiet it down. It’s unwanted, yes. I don’t feel like I’m choosing to keep thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: When the worry comes back, do you do something over and over to try to feel better, like checking, repeating, or counting?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I do counting or repeating exactly. I mean, I do look back at my calendar sometimes, or I’ll glance at a note again, but that’s sort of what I’ve always done to keep myself organized.\n\nMy daughter bought me this very pretty planner last Christmas—one of those with stickers in the back, which seems a little silly at my age, but I actually liked it—and I’ll check it, yes, but I’m not sure it’s “over and over” in the way you mean. It’s more like I’m trying to reassure myself I haven’t missed something.\n\nDoctor: Are you worrying about lots of different parts of your life, or mainly about one particular problem?\n\nPatient: It’s not just one thing, no. It sort of wanders. I’ll start thinking about the exhibition schedule—there’s a little community art thing I’ve been helping keep track of—and then somehow I’m worrying about my daughter’s travel plans, whether she’ll get stuck somewhere, and then the gutters on the house because I noticed leaves up there the other day.\n\nIt’s like each thing by itself is small, but in my head it starts to feel as if it could turn into a much bigger mess if I don’t stay on top of it.\n\nDoctor: Did this worrying start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it did. It’s been about… seven months now, give or take. It started not long after my friend died. We had been working on a few things together—little projects, nothing grand, but they mattered to us—and I sort of tried to keep them moving after she was gone.\n\nAt first I thought I was just being responsible, you know, keeping lists and not letting things drop. She was always the one who remembered who needed to be called, which folder had what in it—funny the things you miss. But after that, the worrying just seemed to settle in and not really let up.\n\nDoctor: When the worry hits, do you feel driven to check your planner or notes again even when you know you’ve already checked enough?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “driven,” exactly. Sometimes I do look again, but it’s hard to tell where being careful ends and… well, where it becomes too much.\n\nI’ve always been a notes person. Even in college I had those color-coded tabs—though now I can never find the color I want, which is ridiculous because I bought a whole pack of them. But yes, I’ll sometimes look back at the planner after I’ve already looked. I don’t always feel like I know whether that’s because I need to, or because I just don’t trust my memory in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel mostly connected to your friend’s death, or do they now feel separate from it?\n\nPatient: I think it started around her death, yes, but now it doesn’t always feel tied to that directly. It’s more like my mind just keeps roaming—from the exhibition schedule to my daughter’s travel plans to whether the gutters need fixing.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, well, if I don’t keep an eye on this one little thing, maybe it turns into a much bigger mess. Which sounds silly when I say it, because gutters are gutters, not… I don’t know, a catastrophe. Like things could unravel. I know that sounds a bit dramatic when I hear myself say it.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to control or stop it?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s really the thing that made me come in. Once it starts, I have a very hard time shutting it off.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “You’ve made the list, you checked the dates, you’ve done what you can,” and for a little while that sounds reasonable. But then my mind sort of circles back anyway. It’s like when I used to pack for trips and I’d check the stove twice—although now I’m not even traveling much, so that’s not quite the same—but it’s that same feeling of not being able to leave it alone. Even when I know, logically, there isn’t anything more to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Even when I know, logically, there isn’t anything more to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Not pacing the halls exactly, but I do feel… wound up, even just sitting in my own living room.\n\nI’ll be on the phone and notice I’m tapping my fingers, or I’ll get up to straighten a stack of papers or move a cushion, not because it really needs doing. It’s more that sitting quietly makes me feel jumpy. Funny, I used to enjoy just sitting with a cup of tea and looking out at the garden, and now I’ll suddenly think the books on the side table look crooked. But yes—on edge is probably the right word.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get tired easily?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even if I’ve slept right through the night, I’ll wake up feeling like I’ve already done half a day’s work, which is ridiculous because I’ve only made tea and fed myself breakfast.\n\nAnd then little things tire me out more than they used to. I’ll water the garden beds—nothing strenuous, just moving the hose about—or I’ll sort some papers for book club, and afterward I need to sit down and sort of… gather myself. I used to be able to do those things without even thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I read the newspaper most mornings, and sometimes I realize I’ve read the same paragraph twice, but then again the paper is so full of the same unpleasant stories lately that maybe I’m just avoiding it.\n\nI haven’t noticed it as a major thing, I don’t think. If I’m doing something straightforward, like making a grocery list or checking the mail, I manage. Though I did put the stamps in the wrong drawer last week—but that’s hardly new for me. My sister used to tease me about that years ago. So… I suppose I’d say not especially, unless you mean something more specific.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having a lot of muscle tension?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially by the end of the day. My shoulders and neck get terribly tight, like I’ve been holding myself braced without realizing it.\n\nSometimes I’ll be sitting there reading, or thinking through things for the garden center activities, and I catch myself clenching my jaw. Then I think, goodness, why am I doing that? I suppose it’s like when you hold a handbag too long on one shoulder and don’t notice until afterward—though I’ve always preferred a smaller bag for that reason. But yes, the tightness is mostly in my neck and shoulders.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

I’m not sure I’d say “trouble,” exactly. I do go to bed and I have my little routine — I like the lamp on the left side because the other one flickers a bit, though I keep meaning to replace the bulb — and usually I manage well enough.

Some nights are better than others, I suppose. I might lie there a bit longer than I’d like, but I haven’t really kept track of it. My neighbor used to write down her sleep in a notebook, but that always seemed like it might make you think about it more, if you see what I mean.

So… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed it as a big problem.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is that I can’t seem to turn off the worrying once it gets going. I’ll tell myself, “All right, I’ve made the list, I checked the dates, I did what I could,” and then somehow my mind just keeps circling back anyway.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to be very organized—color-coded calendars, little notes on the fridge, all that—and I still do those things, but lately it doesn’t seem to settle me the way it used to. I know I’ve done what’s reasonable, but I can’t quite make myself stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts keep coming back, do they feel unwanted and intrusive, like they pop into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so—well, maybe “intrusive” is the right word, though it’s not like a flash of something bizarre. It’s more that once the worry starts, I can’t seem to shut it off. I’ll say to myself, I made the list, I checked the dates, I’ve done everything reasonable… and then there it is again, circling back.\n\nI used to keep everything very tidy that way—my husband used to tease me about my little notes—but now the notes don’t really quiet it down. It’s unwanted, yes. I don’t feel like I’m choosing to keep thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: When the worry comes back, do you do something over and over to try to feel better, like checking, repeating, or counting?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I do counting or repeating exactly. I mean, I do look back at my calendar sometimes, or I’ll glance at a note again, but that’s sort of what I’ve always done to keep myself organized.\n\nMy daughter bought me this very pretty planner last Christmas—one of those with stickers in the back, which seems a little silly at my age, but I actually liked it—and I’ll check it, yes, but I’m not sure it’s “over and over” in the way you mean. It’s more like I’m trying to reassure myself I haven’t missed something.\n\nDoctor: Are you worrying about lots of different parts of your life, or mainly about one particular problem?\n\nPatient: It’s not just one thing, no. It sort of wanders. I’ll start thinking about the exhibition schedule—there’s a little community art thing I’ve been helping keep track of—and then somehow I’m worrying about my daughter’s travel plans, whether she’ll get stuck somewhere, and then the gutters on the house because I noticed leaves up there the other day.\n\nIt’s like each thing by itself is small, but in my head it starts to feel as if it could turn into a much bigger mess if I don’t stay on top of it.\n\nDoctor: Did this worrying start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it did. It’s been about… seven months now, give or take. It started not long after my friend died. We had been working on a few things together—little projects, nothing grand, but they mattered to us—and I sort of tried to keep them moving after she was gone.\n\nAt first I thought I was just being responsible, you know, keeping lists and not letting things drop. She was always the one who remembered who needed to be called, which folder had what in it—funny the things you miss. But after that, the worrying just seemed to settle in and not really let up.\n\nDoctor: When the worry hits, do you feel driven to check your planner or notes again even when you know you’ve already checked enough?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “driven,” exactly. Sometimes I do look again, but it’s hard to tell where being careful ends and… well, where it becomes too much.\n\nI’ve always been a notes person. Even in college I had those color-coded tabs—though now I can never find the color I want, which is ridiculous because I bought a whole pack of them. But yes, I’ll sometimes look back at the planner after I’ve already looked. I don’t always feel like I know whether that’s because I need to, or because I just don’t trust my memory in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel mostly connected to your friend’s death, or do they now feel separate from it?\n\nPatient: I think it started around her death, yes, but now it doesn’t always feel tied to that directly. It’s more like my mind just keeps roaming—from the exhibition schedule to my daughter’s travel plans to whether the gutters need fixing.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, well, if I don’t keep an eye on this one little thing, maybe it turns into a much bigger mess. Which sounds silly when I say it, because gutters are gutters, not… I don’t know, a catastrophe. Like things could unravel. I know that sounds a bit dramatic when I hear myself say it.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to control or stop it?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s really the thing that made me come in. Once it starts, I have a very hard time shutting it off.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “You’ve made the list, you checked the dates, you’ve done what you can,” and for a little while that sounds reasonable. But then my mind sort of circles back anyway. It’s like when I used to pack for trips and I’d check the stove twice—although now I’m not even traveling much, so that’s not quite the same—but it’s that same feeling of not being able to leave it alone. Even when I know, logically, there isn’t anything more to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. But in my head they all start to feel connected somehow, like things could unravel if I’m not careful.\n\nDoctor: For the past six months or more, have you been worrying on most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I’d say. It’s not always the same worry, which makes it hard to pin down. My mind just sort of roams—one minute it’s the exhibition schedule, then it’s my daughter’s travel plans, then I’m thinking about whether the house gutters need fixing.\n\nAnd it starts small, but then I get this feeling that if I don’t stay on top of it, it could turn into something much bigger. Like things could unravel. I know that sounds a bit dramatic when I hear myself say it.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to control or stop it?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s really the thing that made me come in. Once it starts, I have a very hard time shutting it off.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “You’ve made the list, you checked the dates, you’ve done what you can,” and for a little while that sounds reasonable. But then my mind sort of circles back anyway. It’s like when I used to pack for trips and I’d check the stove twice—although now I’m not even traveling much, so that’s not quite the same—but it’s that same feeling of not being able to leave it alone. Even when I know, logically, there isn’t anything more to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Not pacing the halls exactly, but I do feel… wound up, even just sitting in my own living room.\n\nI’ll be on the phone and notice I’m tapping my fingers, or I’ll get up to straighten a stack of papers or move a cushion, not because it really needs doing. It’s more that sitting quietly makes me feel jumpy. Funny, I used to enjoy just sitting with a cup of tea and looking out at the garden, and now I’ll suddenly think the books on the side table look crooked. But yes—on edge is probably the right word.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get tired easily?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even if I’ve slept right through the night, I’ll wake up feeling like I’ve already done half a day’s work, which is ridiculous because I’ve only made tea and fed myself breakfast.\n\nAnd then little things tire me out more than they used to. I’ll water the garden beds—nothing strenuous, just moving the hose about—or I’ll sort some papers for book club, and afterward I need to sit down and sort of… gather myself. I used to be able to do those things without even thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I read the newspaper most mornings, and sometimes I realize I’ve read the same paragraph twice, but then again the paper is so full of the same unpleasant stories lately that maybe I’m just avoiding it.\n\nI haven’t noticed it as a major thing, I don’t think. If I’m doing something straightforward, like making a grocery list or checking the mail, I manage. Though I did put the stamps in the wrong drawer last week—but that’s hardly new for me. My sister used to tease me about that years ago. So… I suppose I’d say not especially, unless you mean something more specific.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having a lot of muscle tension?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially by the end of the day. My shoulders and neck get terribly tight, like I’ve been holding myself braced without realizing it.\n\nSometimes I’ll be sitting there reading, or thinking through things for the garden center activities, and I catch myself clenching my jaw. Then I think, goodness, why am I doing that? I suppose it’s like when you hold a handbag too long on one shoulder and don’t notice until afterward—though I’ve always preferred a smaller bag for that reason. But yes, the tightness is mostly in my neck and shoulders.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “trouble,” exactly. I do go to bed and I have my little routine — I like the lamp on the left side because the other one flickers a bit, though I keep meaning to replace the bulb — and usually I manage well enough.\n\nSome nights are better than others, I suppose. I might lie there a bit longer than I’d like, but I haven’t really kept track of it. My neighbor used to write down her sleep in a notebook, but that always seemed like it might make you think about it more, if you see what I mean.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed it as a big problem.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I’ll say to myself, I made the list, I checked the dates, I’ve done everything reasonable… and then there it is again, circling back.\n\nI used to keep everything very tidy that way—my husband used to tease me about my little notes—but now the notes don’t really quiet it down. It’s unwanted, yes. I don’t feel like I’m choosing to keep thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: When the worry comes back, do you do something over and over to try to feel better, like checking, repeating, or counting?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I do counting or repeating exactly. I mean, I do look back at my calendar sometimes, or I’ll glance at a note again, but that’s sort of what I’ve always done to keep myself organized.\n\nMy daughter bought me this very pretty planner last Christmas—one of those with stickers in the back, which seems a little silly at my age, but I actually liked it—and I’ll check it, yes, but I’m not sure it’s “over and over” in the way you mean. It’s more like I’m trying to reassure myself I haven’t missed something.\n\nDoctor: Are you worrying about lots of different parts of your life, or mainly about one particular problem?\n\nPatient: It’s not just one thing, no. It sort of wanders. I’ll start thinking about the exhibition schedule—there’s a little community art thing I’ve been helping keep track of—and then somehow I’m worrying about my daughter’s travel plans, whether she’ll get stuck somewhere, and then the gutters on the house because I noticed leaves up there the other day.\n\nIt’s like each thing by itself is small, but in my head it starts to feel as if it could turn into a much bigger mess if I don’t stay on top of it.\n\nDoctor: Did this worrying start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it did. It’s been about… seven months now, give or take. It started not long after my friend died. We had been working on a few things together—little projects, nothing grand, but they mattered to us—and I sort of tried to keep them moving after she was gone.\n\nAt first I thought I was just being responsible, you know, keeping lists and not letting things drop. She was always the one who remembered who needed to be called, which folder had what in it—funny the things you miss. But after that, the worrying just seemed to settle in and not really let up.\n\nDoctor: When the worry hits, do you feel driven to check your planner or notes again even when you know you’ve already checked enough?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “driven,” exactly. Sometimes I do look again, but it’s hard to tell where being careful ends and… well, where it becomes too much.\n\nI’ve always been a notes person. Even in college I had those color-coded tabs—though now I can never find the color I want, which is ridiculous because I bought a whole pack of them. But yes, I’ll sometimes look back at the planner after I’ve already looked. I don’t always feel like I know whether that’s because I need to, or because I just don’t trust my memory in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel mostly connected to your friend’s death, or do they now feel separate from it?\n\nPatient: I think it started around her death, yes, but now it doesn’t always feel tied to that directly. It’s more like my mind just keeps roaming—from the exhibition schedule to my daughter’s travel plans to whether the gutters need fixing.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, well, if I don’t keep an eye on this one little thing, maybe it turns into a much bigger mess. Which sounds silly when I say it, because gutters are gutters, not… I don’t know, a catastrophe. But in my head they all start to feel connected somehow, like things could unravel if I’m not careful.\n\nDoctor: For the past six months or more, have you been worrying on most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I’d say. It’s not always the same worry, which makes it hard to pin down. My mind just sort of roams—one minute it’s the exhibition schedule, then it’s my daughter’s travel plans, then I’m thinking about whether the house gutters need fixing.\n\nAnd it starts small, but then I get this feeling that if I don’t stay on top of it, it could turn into something much bigger. Like things could unravel. I know that sounds a bit dramatic when I hear myself say it.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to control or stop it?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s really the thing that made me come in. Once it starts, I have a very hard time shutting it off.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “You’ve made the list, you checked the dates, you’ve done what you can,” and for a little while that sounds reasonable. But then my mind sort of circles back anyway. It’s like when I used to pack for trips and I’d check the stove twice—although now I’m not even traveling much, so that’s not quite the same—but it’s that same feeling of not being able to leave it alone. Even when I know, logically, there isn’t anything more to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Not pacing the halls exactly, but I do feel… wound up, even just sitting in my own living room.\n\nI’ll be on the phone and notice I’m tapping my fingers, or I’ll get up to straighten a stack of papers or move a cushion, not because it really needs doing. It’s more that sitting quietly makes me feel jumpy. Funny, I used to enjoy just sitting with a cup of tea and looking out at the garden, and now I’ll suddenly think the books on the side table look crooked. But yes—on edge is probably the right word.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get tired easily?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even if I’ve slept right through the night, I’ll wake up feeling like I’ve already done half a day’s work, which is ridiculous because I’ve only made tea and fed myself breakfast.\n\nAnd then little things tire me out more than they used to. I’ll water the garden beds—nothing strenuous, just moving the hose about—or I’ll sort some papers for book club, and afterward I need to sit down and sort of… gather myself. I used to be able to do those things without even thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I read the newspaper most mornings, and sometimes I realize I’ve read the same paragraph twice, but then again the paper is so full of the same unpleasant stories lately that maybe I’m just avoiding it.\n\nI haven’t noticed it as a major thing, I don’t think. If I’m doing something straightforward, like making a grocery list or checking the mail, I manage. Though I did put the stamps in the wrong drawer last week—but that’s hardly new for me. My sister used to tease me about that years ago. So… I suppose I’d say not especially, unless you mean something more specific.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having a lot of muscle tension?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially by the end of the day. My shoulders and neck get terribly tight, like I’ve been holding myself braced without realizing it.\n\nSometimes I’ll be sitting there reading, or thinking through things for the garden center activities, and I catch myself clenching my jaw. Then I think, goodness, why am I doing that? I suppose it’s like when you hold a handbag too long on one shoulder and don’t notice until afterward—though I’ve always preferred a smaller bag for that reason. But yes, the tightness is mostly in my neck and shoulders.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “trouble,” exactly. I do go to bed and I have my little routine — I like the lamp on the left side because the other one flickers a bit, though I keep meaning to replace the bulb — and usually I manage well enough.\n\nSome nights are better than others, I suppose. I might lie there a bit longer than I’d like, but I haven’t really kept track of it. My neighbor used to write down her sleep in a notebook, but that always seemed like it might make you think about it more, if you see what I mean.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed it as a big problem.\n\nDoctor: When you check your planner or notes again, are you doing it to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I suppose, yes, in a way. Not that I think the house will fall down if I don’t look at my planner again, but once the worrying starts, I can’t seem to switch it off.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “You’ve made the list, you checked the dates, there’s nothing more to do,” and then five minutes later I’m looking again anyway. It’s a bit like when you leave the iron and wonder if you unplugged it, though I don’t really iron much anymore—most things don’t need it if you hang them properly. But yes, it’s that feeling of needing to be sure, even when I know I’ve done all I reasonably can.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Yes… I think that’s it. Part of me knows I’ve already made the list and checked the dates, and there isn’t anything more I can sensibly do.

So… I suppose I’d say not especially, unless you mean something more specific.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having a lot of muscle tension?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially by the end of the day. My shoulders and neck get terribly tight, like I’ve been holding myself braced without realizing it.\n\nSometimes I’ll be sitting there reading, or thinking through things for the garden center activities, and I catch myself clenching my jaw. Then I think, goodness, why am I doing that? I suppose it’s like when you hold a handbag too long on one shoulder and don’t notice until afterward—though I’ve always preferred a smaller bag for that reason. But yes, the tightness is mostly in my neck and shoulders.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “trouble,” exactly. I do go to bed and I have my little routine — I like the lamp on the left side because the other one flickers a bit, though I keep meaning to replace the bulb — and usually I manage well enough.\n\nSome nights are better than others, I suppose. I might lie there a bit longer than I’d like, but I haven’t really kept track of it. My neighbor used to write down her sleep in a notebook, but that always seemed like it might make you think about it more, if you see what I mean.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed it as a big problem.\n\nDoctor: When you check your planner or notes again, are you doing it to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I suppose, yes, in a way. Not that I think the house will fall down if I don’t look at my planner again, but once the worrying starts, I can’t seem to switch it off.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “You’ve made the list, you checked the dates, there’s nothing more to do,” and then five minutes later I’m looking again anyway. It’s a bit like when you leave the iron and wonder if you unplugged it, though I don’t really iron much anymore—most things don’t need it if you hang them properly. But yes, it’s that feeling of needing to be sure, even when I know I’ve done all I reasonably can.\n\nDoctor: When you look at your planner or notes again, does it feel like something you have to do, even though part of you knows it probably isn’t necessary?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s it. Part of me knows I’ve already made the list and checked the dates, and there isn’t anything more I can sensibly do.\n\nBut once the worry starts, it’s very hard to turn it off. I’ll go back to the planner just to settle myself, I suppose, though it doesn’t always settle me for long. It reminds me of when you reread a recipe even though you’ve made it for years—my mother used to do that with sponge cake, funny enough—but yes, with me it’s more that I feel I have to check because the worrying won’t leave me alone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’ll start thinking about the exhibition schedule—there’s a little community art thing I’ve been helping keep track of—and then somehow I’m worrying about my daughter’s travel plans, whether she’ll get stuck somewhere, and then the gutters on the house because I noticed leaves up there the other day.\n\nIt’s like each thing by itself is small, but in my head it starts to feel as if it could turn into a much bigger mess if I don’t stay on top of it.\n\nDoctor: Did this worrying start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it did. Like things could unravel. I know that sounds a bit dramatic when I hear myself say it.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to control or stop it?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s really the thing that made me come in. Once it starts, I have a very hard time shutting it off.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “You’ve made the list, you checked the dates, you’ve done what you can,” and for a little while that sounds reasonable. But then my mind sort of circles back anyway. It’s like when I used to pack for trips and I’d check the stove twice—although now I’m not even traveling much, so that’s not quite the same—but it’s that same feeling of not being able to leave it alone. Even when I know, logically, there isn’t anything more to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Not pacing the halls exactly, but I do feel… wound up, even just sitting in my own living room.\n\nI’ll be on the phone and notice I’m tapping my fingers, or I’ll get up to straighten a stack of papers or move a cushion, not because it really needs doing. It’s more that sitting quietly makes me feel jumpy. Funny, I used to enjoy just sitting with a cup of tea and looking out at the garden, and now I’ll suddenly think the books on the side table look crooked. I’ll start thinking about the exhibition schedule—there’s a little community art thing I’ve been helping keep track of—and then somehow I’m worrying about my daughter’s travel plans, whether she’ll get stuck somewhere, and then the gutters on the house because I noticed leaves up there the other day.\n\nIt’s like each thing by itself is small, but in my head it starts to feel as if it could turn into a much bigger mess if I don’t stay on top of it.\n\nDoctor: Did this worrying start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it did. It’s been about… seven months now, give or take. It started not long after my friend died. We had been working on a few things together—little projects, nothing grand, but they mattered to us—and I sort of tried to keep them moving after she was gone.\n\nAt first I thought I was just being responsible, you know, keeping lists and not letting things drop. She was always the one who remembered who needed to be called, which folder had what in it—funny the things you miss. But after that, the worrying just seemed to settle in and not really let up.\n\nDoctor: When the worry hits, do you feel driven to check your planner or notes again even when you know you’ve already checked enough?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “driven,” exactly. Sometimes I do look again, but it’s hard to tell where being careful ends and… well, where it becomes too much.\n\nI’ve always been a notes person. Even in college I had those color-coded tabs—though now I can never find the color I want, which is ridiculous because I bought a whole pack of them. But yes, I’ll sometimes look back at the planner after I’ve already looked. I don’t always feel like I know whether that’s because I need to, or because I just don’t trust my memory in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel mostly connected to your friend’s death, or do they now feel separate from it?\n\nPatient: I think it started around her death, yes, but now it doesn’t always feel tied to that directly. It’s more like my mind just keeps roaming—from the exhibition schedule to my daughter’s travel plans to whether the gutters need fixing.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, well, if I don’t keep an eye on this one little thing, maybe it turns into a much bigger mess. Which sounds silly when I say it, because gutters are gutters, not… I don’t know, a catastrophe. But in my head they all start to feel connected somehow, like things could unravel if I’m not careful.\n\nDoctor: For the past six months or more, have you been worrying on most days?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I’d say. It’s not always the same worry, which makes it hard to pin down. My mind just sort of roams—one minute it’s the exhibition schedule, then it’s my daughter’s travel plans, then I’m thinking about whether the house gutters need fixing.\n\nAnd it starts small, but then I get this feeling that if I don’t stay on top of it, it could turn into something much bigger. Like things could unravel. I know that sounds a bit dramatic when I hear myself say it.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to control or stop it?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s really the thing that made me come in. Once it starts, I have a very hard time shutting it off.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “You’ve made the list, you checked the dates, you’ve done what you can,” and for a little while that sounds reasonable. But then my mind sort of circles back anyway. It’s like when I used to pack for trips and I’d check the stove twice—although now I’m not even traveling much, so that’s not quite the same—but it’s that same feeling of not being able to leave it alone. Even when I know, logically, there isn’t anything more to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Not pacing the halls exactly, but I do feel… wound up, even just sitting in my own living room.\n\nI’ll be on the phone and notice I’m tapping my fingers, or I’ll get up to straighten a stack of papers or move a cushion, not because it really needs doing. It’s more that sitting quietly makes me feel jumpy. Funny, I used to enjoy just sitting with a cup of tea and looking out at the garden, and now I’ll suddenly think the books on the side table look crooked. But yes—on edge is probably the right word.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get tired easily?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even if I’ve slept right through the night, I’ll wake up feeling like I’ve already done half a day’s work, which is ridiculous because I’ve only made tea and fed myself breakfast.\n\nAnd then little things tire me out more than they used to. I’ll water the garden beds—nothing strenuous, just moving the hose about—or I’ll sort some papers for book club, and afterward I need to sit down and sort of… gather myself. I used to be able to do those things without even thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I read the newspaper most mornings, and sometimes I realize I’ve read the same paragraph twice, but then again the paper is so full of the same unpleasant stories lately that maybe I’m just avoiding it.\n\nI haven’t noticed it as a major thing, I don’t think. If I’m doing something straightforward, like making a grocery list or checking the mail, I manage. Though I did put the stamps in the wrong drawer last week—but that’s hardly new for me. My sister used to tease me about that years ago. So… I suppose I’d say not especially, unless you mean something more specific.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having a lot of muscle tension?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially by the end of the day. My shoulders and neck get terribly tight, like I’ve been holding myself braced without realizing it.\n\nSometimes I’ll be sitting there reading, or thinking through things for the garden center activities, and I catch myself clenching my jaw. Then I think, goodness, why am I doing that? I suppose it’s like when you hold a handbag too long on one shoulder and don’t notice until afterward—though I’ve always preferred a smaller bag for that reason. But yes, the tightness is mostly in my neck and shoulders.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “trouble,” exactly. I do go to bed and I have my little routine — I like the lamp on the left side because the other one flickers a bit, though I keep meaning to replace the bulb — and usually I manage well enough.\n\nSome nights are better than others, I suppose. I might lie there a bit longer than I’d like, but I haven’t really kept track of it. My neighbor used to write down her sleep in a notebook, but that always seemed like it might make you think about it more, if you see what I mean.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed it as a big problem.\n\nDoctor: When you check your planner or notes again, are you doing it to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I suppose, yes, in a way. Not that I think the house will fall down if I don’t look at my planner again, but once the worrying starts, I can’t seem to switch it off.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “You’ve made the list, you checked the dates, there’s nothing more to do,” and then five minutes later I’m looking again anyway. It’s a bit like when you leave the iron and wonder if you unplugged it, though I don’t really iron much anymore—most things don’t need it if you hang them properly. But yes, it’s that feeling of needing to be sure, even when I know I’ve done all I reasonably can.\n\nDoctor: When you look at your planner or notes again, does it feel like something you have to do, even though part of you knows it probably isn’t necessary?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s it. Part of me knows I’ve already made the list and checked the dates, and there isn’t anything more I can sensibly do.\n\nBut once the worry starts, it’s very hard to turn it off. I’ll go back to the planner just to settle myself, I suppose, though it doesn’t always settle me for long. It reminds me of when you reread a recipe even though you’ve made it for years—my mother used to do that with sponge cake, funny enough—but yes, with me it’s more that I feel I have to check because the worrying won’t leave me alone.\n\nDoctor: About how much time in a day do you spend checking your planner or notes?\n\nPatient: Oh, I’m not really sure. I haven’t timed it or anything like that.\n\nIt’s sort of here and there — I might look once, then get distracted making tea or noticing the mail needs sorting, and then later I’ll glance again. So I couldn’t give you a proper number of minutes. I suppose it depends on the day, really.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

So I couldn’t give you a proper number of minutes. I suppose it depends on the day, really.\n\n[Last Question]\nOn a typical day, does checking your planner or notes take more than an hour altogether?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I could say it adds up to more than an hour. I haven’t really sat with a clock and counted it.

It feels like little bits through the day—look at the planner, then I remember I meant to water that plant in the kitchen, or I see a note about ringing someone and then I’m off thinking about whether it’s too late in the day to call. So… altogether, I’m not sure. It might feel longer than it is, if that makes sense.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is that I can’t seem to turn off the worrying once it gets going. I’ll tell myself, “All right, I’ve made the list, I checked the dates, I did what I could,” and then somehow my mind just keeps circling back anyway.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to be very organized—color-coded calendars, little notes on the fridge, all that—and I still do those things, but lately it doesn’t seem to settle me the way it used to. I know I’ve done what’s reasonable, but I can’t quite make myself stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts keep coming back, do they feel unwanted and intrusive, like they pop into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so—well, maybe “intrusive” is the right word, though it’s not like a flash of something bizarre. It’s more that once the worry starts, I can’t seem to shut it off. I’ll say to myself, I made the list, I checked the dates, I’ve done everything reasonable… and then there it is again, circling back.\n\nI used to keep everything very tidy that way—my husband used to tease me about my little notes—but now the notes don’t really quiet it down. It’s unwanted, yes. I don’t feel like I’m choosing to keep thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: When the worry comes back, do you do something over and over to try to feel better, like checking, repeating, or counting?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I do counting or repeating exactly.
